Asymmetric Distribution of Extreme Values of Cubic L L -functions at s = 1 s=1
Pranendu Darbar, Chantal David, Matilde Lalin, Allysa Lumley

Abstract
. We investigate the distribution of values of cubic Dirichlet L L -functions at s = 1 s=1 . Following ideas of Granville and Soundararajan for quadratic L L -functions, we model the distribution of L ⁡ ( 1 , χ ) L(1,\chi) by the distribution of random Euler products L ⁡ ( 1 , 𝕏 ) L(1,\mathbb{X}) for certain family of random variables 𝕏 ⁡ ( p ) \mathbb{X}(p) attached to each prime. We obtain a description of the proportion of | L ⁡ ( 1 , χ ) | |L(1,\chi)| that are larger or that are smaller than a given bound, and yield more light into the Littlewood bounds. Unlike the quadratic case, there is an asymmetry between lower and upper bounds for the cubic case, and small values are less probable than large values.



2020 Mathematics Subject Classification
11M06, 11R16, 11M20

1.
Introduction

We study in this paper the distribution of the values of Dirichlet
L
-functions attached to cubic characters. Let
\chi
be a primitive cubic Dirichlet character over
{\mathbb{Q}}
, and let

\displaystyle L(s,\chi)=\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}\frac{\chi(n)}{n^{s}}.

We are interested in the distribution of the special values
|L(1,\chi)|
as
\chi
varies over the family
\mathcal{F}_{3}
of cubic primitive characters over
{\mathbb{Q}}
.
The approach of this work is to compare the distribution of values for
|L(1,\chi)|
with the distribution of values of a random Euler product,
|L(1,\mathbb{X})|
, where
L(1,\mathbb{X})=\prod_{p}\left(1-\frac{\mathbb{X}(p)}{p}\right)^{-1}
and the
\mathbb{X}(p)
are independent random variables that take the values
0,1,\omega_{3}:=e^{\frac{2\pi i}{3}}
and
\omega_{3}^{2}
with suitable probabilities.
The main motivation for this work is in determining the extreme values that
|L(1,\chi)|
can take.

This problem has been studied thoroughly in the case of the quadratic characters, with the pioneering work of Granville and Soundararajan
[
GS03
]
describing the distribution of extreme values for
L(1,\chi_{d})
where
\chi_{d}
varies over quadratic characters,
in order to gain understanding of the well-known discrepancy between the extreme values that
L(1,\chi_{d})
may exhibit (the
\Omega
-results of Chowla, described below) and the conditional bounds on these extreme values (the
O
-results of Littlewood).

For a quadratic character
\chi_{d}
of conductor
|d|
, it was shown by Littlewood
[
Lit28
]
(assuming GRH, the Generalized Riemann Hypothesis) that

(1.1)

\displaystyle\left(\frac{1}{2}+o(1)\right)\frac{\zeta(2)}{e^{\gamma}\log_{2}{|d|}}\leq L(1,\chi_{d})\leq(2+o(1))e^{\gamma}\log_{2}{|d|},

where here and throughout,
\gamma
is the Euler-Mascheroni constant, and the notation
\log_{j}
represents the
j
-fold iterated logarithm, so that
\log_{2}|d|=\log\log|d|
.
On the other hand, under the same hypothesis, Littlewood also established that there are infinitely many fundamental discriminants
d
for which

(1.2)

\displaystyle L(1,\chi_{d})\geq(1+o(1))e^{\gamma}\log_{2}{|d|},

and there are infinitely many fundamental discriminants
d
such that

(1.3)

\displaystyle L(1,\chi_{d})\leq(1+o(1))\zeta(2)/(e^{\gamma}\log_{2}{|d|}).

In
[
Cho49
]
, Chowla removed the assumption of GRH on the
\Omega
-result (
1.2
). If we compare the bounds in (
1.1
) with those obtained in (
1.2
) and (
1.3
), we can see there is a discrepancy in the coefficient of the main term. In
[
GS03
]
, the authors provide asymptotics for the probability that
L(1,\chi_{d})>e^{\gamma}\tau
, and for the probability the
L(1,\chi_{d})<\frac{\zeta(2)}{e^{\gamma}\tau}
uniformly in a wide range of
\tau
.
The uniformity of their results provides evidence that the
\Omega
-results of Chowla may represent the true nature of these extreme values, but
falls just short of determining which coefficient is the correct one.

We now turn to the extreme values of cubic characters.
Our first result is a conditional
O
-result in the style of Littlewood for cubic characters.

Proposition 1.1
.

Let
\chi
be a primitive character of order
3
and conductor
q
. Assume GRH for
L(s,\chi)
. Then we have

\left(\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}+o(1)\right)\left(\frac{\zeta(3)}{e^{\gamma}\log_{2}q}\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}\leq|L(1,\chi)|\leq\left(2+o(1)\right)e^{\gamma}\log_{2}q.

The upper bound is not new, as the original proof of Littlewood holds for any primitive character
\chi
of conductor
q
, but to our knowledge, the lower bound does not appear in the literature. Proposition
1.1
is a particular case of Proposition
1.7
which holds for characters of prime order
\ell\geq 3
.
We also obtain
\Omega
-results, namely Theorem
1.5
, which exhibits a constant discrepancy similar to the case of quadratic characters: 1 versus 2 for the upper bound and 1 versus
\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}
for the lower bound. Other
\Omega
-results for the large values of characters of order
\ell\geq 2
as
[
Lam17
, Theorem 1.2]
(for the upper bound) exhibit the same constant discrepancy.

Our main theorems describe the distribution of extreme values for
|L(1,\chi)|
for a family of cubic characters. Limiting distribution results for cubic characters over
{\mathbb{Q}}(\sqrt{-3})
were obtained by Akbary and Hamieh
[
AH20
,
AH21
]
. To our knowledge, our results are the first describing the distribution of extreme values for cubic characters over
{\mathbb{Z}}
, by considering the tail of the distribution, and represent the first family where there is an
asymmetric distribution
.
The origin of this asymmetric distribution is simple: the minimum value of
|L(1,\chi)|
for cubic characters is larger than the corresponding minumum for quadratic characters. This can already be seen at the level of each individual factor in the Euler product, since
\chi(p)=\omega_{3}^{h}
for some
h=0,1,
or
2
and then we have

\left|1-\frac{\chi(p)}{p}\right|=\left(1-\frac{2\cos\left(\frac{2\pi h}{3}\right)}{p}+\frac{1}{p^{2}}\right)^{1/2}.

While the minimum value is achieved at
h=0
, the maximum value is given at
h=1
or
h=2
and therefore

\left|1-\frac{1}{p}\right|\leq\left|1-\frac{\chi(p)}{p}\right|\leq\left|1+\frac{1}{p}+\frac{1}{p^{2}}\right|^{1/2},

which explains the asymmetry.

See also
[
MV07
, Exercise 4, p. 366]
for the unconditional lower bound
|L(1,\chi)|\gg\frac{1}{\sqrt{\log q}}
for
\chi
cubic.

As in
[
GS03
]
, the range of uniformity we are able to achieve leads us to believe that the
\Omega
-results of Theorem
1.5
may represent the true nature of the extreme values in cubic families.

In order to precisely describe our results, we need some notation. For
X
large, let

\mathcal{F}_{3}(X)
denote the subset of
\mathcal{F}_{3}
of cubic characters with conductor bounded by
X
and let

\displaystyle\phi_{X}(\tau)

\displaystyle:={\mathbb{P}}\left(|L(1,\chi)|>e^{\gamma}\tau\right):=\frac{1}{|\mathcal{F}_{3}(X)|}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\chi\in\mathcal{F}_{3}(X)\\
|L(1,\chi)|>e^{\gamma}\tau\end{subarray}}1

\displaystyle\psi_{X}(\tau)

\displaystyle:={\mathbb{P}}\left(|L(1,\chi)|<\left(\frac{\zeta(3)}{e^{\gamma}}\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}\frac{1}{\tau}\right):=\frac{1}{|\mathcal{F}_{3}(X)|}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\chi\in\mathcal{F}_{3}(X)\\
|L(1,\chi)|<\left(\frac{\zeta(3)}{e^{\gamma}}\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}\frac{1}{\tau}\end{subarray}}1.

To study these probabilities, we use the method of moments, and we show that the
complex moments of
|L(1,\chi)|
agree with the expectations of random Euler products
L(1,\mathbb{X})

for a large range of values.

We now define the independent random variables
{\mathbb{X}}(p)
given by

(1.4)

\mathbb{X}(p)=\begin{cases}1&\mbox{with probability}=\frac{1}{3},\\
\omega_{3}&\mbox{with probability}=\frac{1}{3},\\
\omega_{3}^{2}&\mbox{with probability}=\frac{1}{3},\end{cases}

when
p\equiv 2\,(\mathrm{mod}\,3)
or
p=3
, and by

(1.5)

\mathbb{X}(p)=\begin{cases}0&\mbox{with probability}=\frac{2}{p+2},\\
1&\mbox{with probability}=\frac{p}{3(p+2)},\\
\omega_{3}&\mbox{with probability}=\frac{p}{3(p+2)},\\
\omega_{3}^{2}&\mbox{with probability}=\frac{p}{3(p+2)},\end{cases}

when
p\equiv 1\,(\mathrm{mod}\,3)
.
We will see in Section 2 why these random variables are naturally associated to the cubic family
\mathcal{F}_{3}
.

Let
n=p_{1}^{a_{1}}p_{2}^{a_{2}}\cdots p_{k}^{a_{k}}
be the prime power factorization of
n
. We extend the definition of
\mathbb{X}
by multiplicativity

\mathbb{X}(n)=\mathbb{X}(p_{1})^{a_{1}}\mathbb{X}(p_{2})^{a_{2}}\cdots\mathbb{X}(p_{k})^{a_{k}},

and we define

\displaystyle L(1,\mathbb{X})=\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}\frac{\mathbb{X}(n)}{n}=\prod_{p\text{ prime}}\left(1-\frac{\mathbb{X}(p)}{p}\right)^{-1},

where both the series and the product are almost surely convergent by Lemma
4.2
.
Before describing how well this model approximates the distribution of
|L(1,\chi)|
, it is useful to understand the behaviour of the distribution of
|L(1,\mathbb{X})|
. To this end, we define, for
\tau>0
,

(1.6)

\displaystyle\Phi(\tau)

\displaystyle:={\mathbb{P}}\left(|L(1,{\mathbb{X}})|>e^{\gamma}\tau\right),

(1.7)

\displaystyle\Psi(\tau)

\displaystyle:={\mathbb{P}}\left(|L(1,{\mathbb{X}})|<\left(\frac{\zeta(3)}{e^{\gamma}}\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}\frac{1}{\tau}\right).

We obtain the following asymptotic behaviour for
\Phi(\tau)
and
\Psi(\tau)
, which are each decaying doubly exponentially, although with different rates:

Theorem 1.2
.

For large
\tau
, we have

\displaystyle\Phi(\tau)=\exp\left(-\frac{2e^{\tau-C_{\max}}}{\tau}\left(1+O(\tau^{-1})\right)\right)

and

\Psi(\tau)=\exp\bigg(-\frac{e^{\tau^{2}-C_{\min}}}{\tau^{2}}\left(1+O(\tau^{-1})\right)\bigg),

where
C_{\max}\approx 0.98727\dots
and
C_{\min}\approx 1.40459\dots
are defined in (
4.7
) and (
4.8
) respectively.

We then show that the distribution of
L(1,\chi)
over
\chi\in\mathcal{F}_{3}(X)
is well approximated by the distribution for the random Euler product
L(1,{\mathbb{X}})
, in a large range of
\tau
.

Theorem 1.3
.

Let
X
be large.
Then, uniformly in the range
1\leq\tau\leq\log_{2}X-\log_{3}X-\log_{4}X-2
, we have

\displaystyle\phi_{X}(\tau)

\displaystyle=\exp\left(-\frac{2e^{\tau-C_{\max}}}{\tau}\left(1+O(\tau^{-1/2})\right)\right),

and uniformly in the range
1\leq\tau\leq\sqrt{\log_{2}X-\log_{3}X-\log_{4}X-2}
,

\displaystyle\psi_{X}(\tau)

\displaystyle=\exp\left(-\frac{e^{\tau^{2}-C_{\min}}}{\tau^{2}}\left(1+O(\tau^{-1/2})\right)\right),

where
C_{\max}
and
C_{\min}
are as in Theorem
1.2
.

If we compare Theorem
1.3
with the other distribution and extreme values results found in the literature, for families of quadratic characters
[
GS03
,
DL18
,
Lum19
]
, for
|\zeta(1+it)|

[
GS06
]
, for all characters modulo
q

[
LLS15
]
, or for
L
-functions of automorphic forms on
\mathrm{GL}_{n}

[
Lum18
]
, we see that the probability distributions for cubic characters are different,
as
\phi_{X}(\tau)
and
\psi_{X}(\tau)
decay with different rates. Since
\psi_{X}(\tau)
decays faster, the small values are less probable, which is reflected in the
O
-results and
\Omega
-results for small values of cubic characters, in Proposition
1.1
and Theorem
1.5
. The constants
C_{\max}
and
C_{\min}
are also different than the constant
C_{1}
which appears consistently in the articles on quadratic families mentioned above.

We remark that the ranges of uniformity for
\phi_{X}(\tau)
and
\psi_{X}(\tau)
in Theorem
1.3
are comparable to
[
GS03
, Theorem 1]
, taking into account the increased rate of decay of Theorem
1.2
in the case of
\Psi(\tau)
.

Theorem
1.3
can be improved under GRH.

Theorem 1.4
.

Assume GRH for Hecke
L
-functions over
{\mathbb{Q}}(\omega_{3})
.
Let
X
be large and let
e^{10}\leq A\leq(\log_{2}X)^{C}
be a real number. Then, uniformly in the range
\tau\leq\log_{2}X+\log_{3}X-\log_{2}A-37+C_{\max}+o(1)
, we have

\displaystyle\phi_{X}(\tau)=\exp\left(-\frac{2e^{\tau-C_{\max}}}{\tau}\left(1+O(\tau^{-1/2}+A^{-1})\right)\right)

and, uniformly in the range
\tau\leq\sqrt{\log_{2}X+\log_{3}X-\log_{2}A-37+C_{\min}+o(1)}
, we have

\psi_{X}(\tau)=\exp\left(-\frac{e^{\tau^{2}-C_{\min}}}{\tau^{2}}\left(1+O(\tau^{-1/2}+A^{-1})\right)\right),

where
C_{\max}
and
C_{\min}
are defined in (
4.7
) and (
4.8
) respectively.

The above result gives an asymptotic formula if
A=A(X)
is chosen as an arbitrary function of
X
such that
A(X)\rightarrow\infty
as
X\rightarrow\infty
.

We now move to the
\Omega
-results.

Theorem 1.5

(Detecting maximum and minimum values)
.

Assume GRH for Hecke
L
-functions over
{\mathbb{Q}}(\omega_{3})
. For
X
large, there are
\gg X^{\frac{1}{2}}
cubic characters with prime conductor bounded by
X
such that

(1.8)

|L(1,\chi)|\geq e^{\gamma}\left(\log_{2}X+\log_{3}X-\log(2\log 3)+o(1)\right).

For
X
large, there are
\gg X^{\frac{1}{2}}
cubic characters with prime conductor bounded by
X
such that

(1.9)

|L(1,\chi)|\leq\frac{\zeta(3)^{\frac{1}{2}}}{(e^{\gamma}\log_{2}X+\log_{3}X-\log(2\log 3)+o(1))^{\frac{1}{2}}}.

Other
\Omega
-results for the large values of characters of order
3
can be found in
[
Lam17
, Theorem 2.1]
, where the
proportion of characters is
X^{1-\epsilon}
, but the lower bound is smaller, containing only the first term
\log_{2}{X}
. We are able to get an additional
\log_{3}{X}
because we are using the distribution. We are then led to the following conjecture about the precise size of the maximal and minimal order for
L
-functions associated with cubic characters, which is the equivalent of a conjecture of
[
GS03
]
for quadratic characters, refining the conjectures of
[
MV99
]

[
GS03
, Conjecture 2]
. A similar conjecture for the large values of
|\zeta(1+it)|
can be found in
[
GS06
]
. For characters of order
\ell\geq 3
, a conjecture for the leading term
\log_{2}X
for the large values can be found in

[
Lam17
]
.

Conjecture 1.6
.

Let
\mathcal{F}_{3}(X)
be the family of primitive cubic characters over
{\mathbb{Q}}
with conductor bounded by
X
. Then,

\max_{\chi\in\mathcal{F}_{3}(X)}|L(1,\chi)|=e^{\gamma}(\log_{2}X+\log_{3}X+C_{\max}-\log 2+o(1)),

and

\min_{\chi\in\mathcal{F}_{3}(X)}|L(1,\chi)|=\left(\frac{\zeta(3)}{e^{\gamma}(\log_{2}X+\log_{3}X+C_{\min}+o(1))}\right)^{\frac{1}{2}},

where
C_{\max}
and
C_{\min}
are as in Theorem
1.2
.

We reach this conjecture by assuming that the range for the upper bound (respectively lower bound) in Theorem
1.4
can be sufficiently extended so that one can replace the value of
\tau
in the expression for
\phi_{X}(\tau)
(resp.
\psi_{X}(\tau)
) by
\tau_{\max}=\log_{2}X+\log_{3}X+C_{\max}-\log 2+\delta
(resp.
\tau_{\min}=\sqrt{\log_{2}X+\log_{3}X+C_{\min}+\delta}
) for some constant
\delta\geq 0
. This results in

\phi_{X}(\tau_{\max})\leq X^{-e^{\delta}}\left(1+o(1)\right),\qquad\psi_{X}(\tau_{\min})\leq X^{-e^{\delta}}\left(1+o(1)\right).

Since
X^{-e^{\delta}}<\frac{1}{X}
for
\delta>0
and the total number of characters is of order
X
, this suggests that we do not cross the barrier of
\delta>0
when considering
\max_{\chi\in\mathcal{F}_{3}(X)}|L(1,\chi)|
and
\min_{\chi\in\mathcal{F}_{3}(X)}|L(1,\chi)|
.

Furthermore, Theorem
1.5
comes (under GRH) very close to exhibiting
X^{\frac{1}{2}}
characters such that
|L(1,\chi)|
is close to the extreme values predicted by Conjecture
1.6
, since
-\log{(2\log 3)}=-0.78719\dots
and
C_{\max}-\log 2=0.29412\dots
. The same observation (with the appropriate modifications) holds for the small values in Theorem
1.5
.

Notice that our results in Theorem
1.4
prove (under GRH) some conjectures that are cubic analogues to those of Montgomery and Vaughan
[
MV99
]
(more precisely, Theorem
1.4
implies an analogue of Conjecture 1 and the upper bound on Conjecture 2 in the notation of
[
GS03
]
). Namely, using the maximal allowable value
\tau
in Theorem
1.4
implies that
the proportion of characters
\chi\in\mathcal{F}_{3}(X)
such that

|L(1,\chi)|>e^{\gamma}\left(\log_{2}X+\log_{3}X-\log_{2}A-37+C_{\max}+o(1)\right)

is

\exp\left(-\frac{2e^{\log_{2}X+\log_{3}X-\log_{2}A-37+o(1)}}{\log_{2}X}\right).

If we replace by
\tau=\log_{2}X
(which is in the range of Theorem
1.4
), we get that the proportion of characters
\chi\in\mathcal{F}_{3}(X)
such that
|L(1,\chi)|>e^{\gamma}\log_{2}X
is
both
>\exp\left(-C\frac{\log{X}}{\log_{2}X}\right)
and
<\exp\left(-c\frac{\log{X}}{\log_{2}X}\right)
for some appropriate constants
0<c<C<\infty
, thus leading (under GRH) to a cubic analogue of Conjecture 1 in
[
GS03
]
.

Similarly, if we take
\tau=\log_{2}X+\log_{3}X-\log_{2}A-37+C_{\max}
with
A
to be a very large constant (that is, sufficiently large to absorb the constant in the error term, but still a constant) then we conclude that the proportion of characters
\chi\in\mathcal{F}_{3}(X)
such that
|L(1,\chi)|>e^{\gamma}(\log_{2}X+\log_{3}X)
is bounded above by
X^{\Theta}
with some
-1<\Theta<0
, and this leads to the upper bound of the corresponding cubic analogue to Conjecture 2 in
[
GS03
]
.

Less
attention has been paid to the small values in the literature, maybe because the arguments are completely symmetric to the large values for quadratic characters. However, as we have observed in Proposition
1.1
and Theorems
1.2
,
1.3
,
1.4
, and
1.5
, we see an asymmetric behaviour for the family of cubic characters.
Of course, all these results are related: the minimum value of
|L(1,\chi)|
for cubic characters is much larger than what can be found for the quadratic case, which fits perfectly the faster decay for the occurrence of small values of Theorem
1.3
.

There have been several papers working toward proving statements similar to Conjecture
1.6

for different families of
L
-functions, including the work of Bondarenko and Seip
[
BS17
]
(for the Riemann zeta function on the critical line), Aistleitner et al.
[
AMM19
]
(for the Riemann zeta function on the
1
-line) and Aistleitner et al.
[
AMMP19
]
(for
L(1,\chi)
with
\chi
taken over the family of primitive characters modulo
q
), making use of a resonator method.
Again, these results just fall short of confirming the conjectures.

1.1.
Generalizations to any prime order
\ell

Since the distribution of extreme values, and in particular of small values, is very different for the family of cubic characters than other families studied in the literature (as quadratic characters or all character of conductor
q
), it is interesting to speculate on what would happen for characters of prime order
\ell>3
. One can see how most of the results of the present paper could be considered in that context, first by generalizing the sieve of Lemma

3.5
to start the computations, which is not trivial, and which would now take place over
{\mathbb{Q}}(\omega_{\ell})
, creating obvious complications (for example, this is not in general a principal ideal domain). Granville and Lamzouri
[
GL21
]
developed a model which can be applied to number theoretic questions about large values of different families of
L
-functions that can be modelled by almost independent random variables. This model could potentially be applied to describe the distribution of large values of
|L(1,\chi)|
where
\chi
is a character of order
\ell
. We hope to address some of these questions in the future. As a starting point, some of the results of this paper can be easily generalized from cubic to characters of order
\ell
, as the
O
-results of Littlewood
and the
\Omega
-results.
We will prove these statements in Section
5
.

Proposition 1.7
.

Let
\ell
be an odd prime and
\chi
be a character of order
\ell
and conductor
q
. Assume GRH for
L(s,\chi)
. Then we have

\frac{C_{\ell}}{\left(2e^{\gamma}\log_{2}q\right)^{\cos\left(\frac{\pi}{\ell}\right)}}(1+o(1))\leq|L(1,\chi)|\leq 2e^{\gamma}\log_{2}q(1+o(1)),

where

(1.10)

\displaystyle C_{\ell}=\prod_{p}\left[\left(1-\frac{1}{p}\right)^{-\cos\left(\frac{\pi}{\ell}\right)}\left(1+\frac{2\cos\left(\frac{\pi}{\ell}\right)}{p}+\frac{1}{p^{2}}\right)^{-1/2}\right].

Remark 1.8
.

For
\ell=3
, we have
C_{3}=\zeta(3)^{\frac{1}{2}}
, and the bounds

\left(\frac{\zeta(3)}{2e^{\gamma}\log_{2}q}\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}\;(1+o(1))\leq|L(1,\chi)|\leq 2e^{\gamma}\log_{2}q(1+o(1)).

In addition,
\lim_{\ell\rightarrow\infty}C_{\ell}=\zeta(2)
, approaching the original bound of Littlewood.

Remark 1.9
.

The fact that large values of Dirichlet characters
L
-functions at
s=1
behave differently if the character is quadratic or of odd order was also noticed in

[
GS07
]
, where the authors show that the Pólya-Vinogradov bound can be improved for odd order characters. In
[
GS07
, Theorem 1]
, the authors prove that if

\chi
is a primitive character modulo
q
of odd order
g
, then

\displaystyle\max_{x}\left|\sum_{n\leq x}\chi(n)\right|\ll_{g}\sqrt{q}(\log q)^{1-\delta_{g}+o(1)}\text{ with }\delta_{g}=1-\frac{g}{\pi}\sin\frac{\pi}{g}.

The exponent approaches
1
at a rate of
O(1/g^{2})
as
g
grows (which is the Pólya–Vinogradov bound for quadratic characters as
g
grows),
at the same rate that
C_{\ell}
is approaching the lower bound for quadratic characters.

Theorem 1.10

(Detecting minimum value)
.

Let
\ell
be a prime. Assume GRH for Hecke
L
-functions over
{\mathbb{Q}}(\omega_{3})
.
For all large
X
, there are
\gg X^{\frac{1}{2}}
characters of order
\ell
with prime conductor bounded by
X
such that

|L(1,\chi)|\leq\frac{C_{\ell}}{(e^{\gamma}\log_{2}X+\log_{3}X-\log(2\log\ell)+o(1))^{\cos\left(\frac{\pi}{\ell}\right)}},

where
C_{\ell}
is given by (
1.10
).

The
\Omega
-results are obtained by following a pretentious approach of Granville and Soundararajan, forcing the characters
\chi
to minimize the value of
\re(\chi)
. More precisely, we count the characters
\chi
for which
\chi(p)=e^{\frac{(\ell-1)\pi i}{\ell}}
or
e^{\frac{(\ell+1)\pi i}{\ell}}
for the primes
p
essentially below
\log X\log_{2}X
. Thus, the somewhat surprising appearance of the exponent
\cos\left(\frac{\pi}{\ell}\right)
is explained by this
strategy of minimizing
\re(\chi(p))
.

As in the cubic case, there is a discrepancy between the constants of Proposition
1.7
(which is
\left(1/2\right)^{\cos(\pi/\ell)}
) and Theorem
1.10
(which is 1). Interestingly,

\left(1/2\right)^{\cos(\pi/\ell)}\rightarrow 1/2
, which is the discrepancy found for quadratic
L
-functions.
We could speculate that also in this case, the true nature of the extreme values is given by the minimum values of Theorem
1.10
and not the
O
-results,
and

\min_{\chi\in\mathcal{F}_{\ell}(X)}|L(1,\chi)|=\frac{C_{\ell}}{\left(e^{\gamma}(\log_{2}X+\log_{3}X+C_{\text{min},\ell}+o(1))\right)^{\cos\left(\frac{\pi}{\ell}\right)}},

where
C_{\ell}
is given by (
1.10
) and
C_{\text{min},\ell}
is a constant that can be explicitly determined from the corresponding random model.
Computing the distribution would shed more light on this question.
We expect the distribution function in the lower bound
\psi_{X}
for general
\ell
to take the shape

\psi_{X}(\tau)=\exp\bigg(-\frac{e^{\tau^{a(\ell)}-C_{\text{min},\ell}}}{\tau^{a(\ell)}}\left(1+o(1)\right)\bigg).

where
a(\ell)=\left({\cos\left(\frac{\pi}{\ell}\right)}\right)^{-1}
.

The average over the family of Section
2
, which is needed to determine the random variables
{\mathbb{X}}(p)
, can also be done easily for the general family
\mathcal{F}_{\ell}
of characters of order
\ell
, which we will do.

1.2.
Organization of the paper

This article is organized as follows. In Section
2
, we define the family of cubic characters, present the relevant background, and compute the average character value over the family. This leads to the definition of the random variables (
1.4
) and (
1.5
). In Section
3
, we prove Theorem
3.1
, estimating the complex moments in our family with the moments of the random variables. In Section
4
, we prove Theorems
1.2
and
1.3
. We consider
\Omega
-results in Section
5
, where we prove Eq. (
1.8
) in Theorem
1.5
,
Propositions
1.7
, and Theorem
1.10
(Eq. (
1.9
) in Theorem
1.5
is a particular case of Theorem
1.10
).
Finally, we prove Theorem
1.4
in Section
6
.

2.
Average over the character family

Let
\ell
be a fixed odd prime, and let
\mathcal{F}_{\ell}
be the set of primitive characters of order
\ell
over
{\mathbb{Q}}
with conductor coprime to
\ell
, i.e.,

(2.1)

\displaystyle\mathcal{F}_{\ell}=\left\{\chi=\chi_{p_{1}}^{e_{1}}\;\chi_{p_{2}}^{e_{2}}\cdots\chi_{p_{s}}^{e_{s}}\;:\;p_{i}\;\text{distinct primes},\;p_{i}\equiv 1\,(\mathrm{mod}\,\ell),\;e_{i}\in\{1,\dots,\ell-1\}\right\},

where
\chi_{p}
is the
\ell
th residue symbol modulo
p
defined by

\displaystyle\chi_{p}(\alpha)\equiv\alpha^{(p-1)/\ell}\,(\mathrm{mod}\,p).

Since there is no canonical choice of a primitive
\ell
th root of unity modulo
p
in
{\mathbb{Q}}
, there is no canonical choice of
\chi_{p}
versus any
\chi_{p}^{h}
with
1<h\leq\ell-1
.

The conductor of each character
\chi_{p_{1}}^{e_{1}}\;\chi_{p_{2}}^{e_{2}}\cdots\chi_{p_{s}}^{e_{s}}
in
\mathcal{F}_{\ell}
is
p_{1}\cdots p_{s}
, where the
p_{i}
are distinct primes congruent to
1\,(\mathrm{mod}\,\ell)
, and for each
p_{1}\cdots p_{s}
there are
(\ell-1)^{s}
primitive cubic characters with this conductor.
We denote by
\mathcal{F}_{\ell}(X)
the subset of
\mathcal{F}_{\ell}
consisting of characters of conductor
\leq X
.
Let
\omega_{\ell}
denote a primitive
\ell
th root of unity in
{\mathbb{C}}
.

Proposition 2.1
.

We have

\displaystyle\sum_{\chi\in\mathcal{F}_{\ell}(X)}1

\displaystyle=K_{\ell}X+O\left(X^{\frac{\ell+2}{\ell+5}+\epsilon}\right),

and more generally for
m\in{\mathbb{Z}},m\geq 1
and
m
an
\ell
th power,

\displaystyle\sum_{\chi\in\mathcal{F}_{\ell}(X)}\chi(m)

\displaystyle=K_{\ell}\prod_{\begin{subarray}{c}p\mid m\\
p\equiv 1\,(\mathrm{mod}\,\ell)\end{subarray}}\left(\frac{p}{p+\ell-1}\right)X+O\left(\ell^{\omega(m)}X^{\frac{\ell+2}{\ell+5}+\epsilon}\right).

Here

\displaystyle K_{\ell}=r_{\ell}F_{\ell,2}(1),

where
r_{\ell}
is the residue of the Dedekind zeta function of the
\ell
th cyclotomic extension
{\mathbb{Q}}(\omega_{\ell})
at
s=1
and
F_{\ell,2}(s)
is given by (
2.3
).
The power of
X
in the error term can be improved to
O\left(\ell^{\omega(m)}X^{\frac{1}{2}+\epsilon}\right)
assuming the Lindelöf Hypothesis.
Therefore, when
m
is an
\ell
th power,

(2.2)

\displaystyle\frac{1}{|\mathcal{F}_{\ell}(X)|}\sum_{\chi\in\mathcal{F}_{\ell}(X)}\chi(m)

\displaystyle=\prod_{\begin{subarray}{c}p\mid m\\
p\equiv 1\,(\mathrm{mod}\,\ell)\end{subarray}}\left(\frac{p}{p+\ell-1}\right)+O\left(\ell^{\omega(m)}X^{-\frac{3}{\ell+5}+\epsilon}\right),

and the error term can be improved to
O\left(\ell^{\omega(m)}X^{-\frac{1}{2}+\epsilon}\right)
assuming the Lindelöf Hypothesis.

Remark 2.2
.

For the applications in this work, we will specialize to the case
\ell=3
.
The main term for the first result was proven by Cohn
[
Coh54
]
in the cubic case
by using the Dirichlet series

\displaystyle\prod_{p\equiv 1\,(\mathrm{mod}\,3)}\left(1+2p^{-s}\right)=\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}\frac{u(n)}{n^{s}},

where
u(n)
is the number of cubic characters of conductor
n
.

Proof.

Let
a_{\ell}(n)
be the number of primitive characters of order
\ell
and conductor
n
. Then,

\displaystyle\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}\frac{a_{\ell}(n)}{n^{s}}

\displaystyle=\prod_{p\equiv 1\,(\mathrm{mod}\,\ell)}\left(1+\frac{\ell-1}{p^{s}}\right)=\prod_{p\equiv 1\,(\mathrm{mod}\,\ell)}\left(1-\frac{1}{p^{s}}\right)^{-(\ell-1)}F_{\ell,1}(s)

\displaystyle=\zeta_{K}(s)F_{\ell,2}(s),

where
\zeta_{K}(s)
is the Dedekind zeta function of
K={\mathbb{Q}}(\omega_{\ell})
. In other words, we have

\displaystyle\zeta_{K}(s)=\left(1-\frac{1}{\ell^{s}}\right)^{-1}\prod_{p\neq\ell}\left(1-\frac{1}{p^{e_{\ell}(p)s}}\right)^{-\frac{(\ell-1)}{e_{\ell}(p)}},

where
e_{\ell}(p)
is the multiplicative order of
p
modulo
\ell
,

\displaystyle F_{\ell,1}(s)

\displaystyle=\prod_{p\equiv 1\,(\mathrm{mod}\,\ell)}\left(1+\frac{\ell-1}{p^{s}}\right)\left(1-\frac{1}{p^{s}}\right)^{\ell-1},

and

(2.3)

\displaystyle F_{\ell,2}(s)

\displaystyle=F_{\ell,1}(s)\left(1-\frac{1}{\ell^{s}}\right)\prod_{\begin{subarray}{c}p\neq\ell\\
e_{\ell}(p)>1\end{subarray}}\left(1-\frac{1}{p^{e_{\ell}(p)s}}\right)^{\frac{\ell-1}{e_{\ell}(p)}}.

Notice that
F_{\ell,1}(s)
and
F_{\ell,2}(s)
converge absolutely for
\re(s)>\frac{1}{2}+\epsilon
.

For any
\frac{1}{2}+\epsilon\leq\sigma\leq 1
, we will use the bound

(2.4)

\displaystyle|\zeta_{K}\left(\sigma+it\right)|

\displaystyle\ll_{\epsilon}t^{\frac{(\ell-1)(1-\sigma)}{3}+\epsilon}\;\;\;\;\;\text{\cite[cite]{[\@@bibref{}{HB88}{}{}]}},

(2.5)

\displaystyle|\zeta_{K}\left(\sigma+it\right)|

\displaystyle\ll_{\epsilon}t^{\epsilon}\;\;\;\;\;\text{Lindel\"{o}f Hypothesis.}

We apply Perron’s formula

\sum_{n\leq X}a_{\ell}(n)=\frac{1}{2\pi i}\int_{1+\epsilon-iT}^{1+\epsilon+iT}\zeta_{K}(s)F_{\ell,2}(s)\frac{X^{s}}{s}ds+O\left(\frac{X^{1+\epsilon}}{T}\right),

and we move the integral to
s=\frac{1}{2}+\epsilon
.
Completing the above integral over a rectangle with vertices in
\frac{1}{2}+\epsilon\pm iT
,
1+\epsilon\pm iT
and applying the bound (
2.4
), we have, for the horizontal integrals,

\displaystyle\ll

\displaystyle\frac{1}{T}\int_{\frac{1}{2}+\epsilon}^{1+\epsilon}|\zeta_{K}(\sigma+iT)||F_{\ell,2}(\sigma+iT)|X^{\sigma}d\sigma\ll\frac{1}{T}\max_{\frac{1}{2}+\epsilon\leq\sigma\leq 1+\epsilon}T^{\frac{(\ell-1)(1-\sigma)}{3}+\epsilon}X^{\sigma}

(2.6)

\displaystyle\ll

\displaystyle\frac{1}{T}\left(T^{\frac{\ell-1}{6}+\epsilon}X^{\frac{1}{2}+\epsilon}+T^{\epsilon}X\right).

For the integral over
s=\frac{1}{2}+\epsilon
, we have

\displaystyle\ll

\displaystyle\int_{-T}^{T}\left|\zeta_{K}\left(\frac{1}{2}+\epsilon+it\right)\right|\left|F_{\ell,2}\left(\frac{1}{2}+\epsilon+it\right)\right|X^{\frac{1}{2}+\epsilon}\frac{dt}{\left|\frac{1}{2}+\epsilon+it\right|}

(2.7)

\displaystyle\ll

\displaystyle T^{\frac{\ell-1}{6}+\epsilon}X^{\frac{1}{2}+\epsilon}.

Setting
T=X^{\frac{3}{\ell+5}}
, we finally obtain

\displaystyle|\mathcal{F}_{\ell}(X)|=r_{\ell}\,F_{\ell,2}(1)\,X+O\left(X^{\frac{\ell+2}{\ell+5}+\epsilon}\right),

where
r_{\ell}
is the residue of
\zeta_{K}(s)
at
s=1
.

If we assume the Lindelöf Hypothesis, we can use the bound (
2.5
) instead of (
2.4
). Then (
2.6
) and (
2.7
) give a bound of
\ll T^{\epsilon-1}X+T^{\epsilon}X^{\frac{1}{2}+\epsilon}
. Taking
T=X^{\frac{1}{2}}
leads to an error term of
O\left(X^{\frac{1}{2}+\epsilon}\right)
.

Now suppose that
m
is a
\ell
-th power. Then,

\displaystyle\sum_{\chi\in\mathcal{F}_{\ell}(X)}\chi(m)=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\chi\in\mathcal{F}_{\ell}(X)\\
\left(\text{cond}(\chi),m\right)=1\end{subarray}}1=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}n\leq X\\
(n,m)=1\end{subarray}}a_{\ell}(n),

and

\displaystyle\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}n=1\\
(n,m)=1\end{subarray}}^{\infty}\frac{a_{\ell}(n)}{n^{s}}=\prod_{\begin{subarray}{c}p\equiv 1\,(\mathrm{mod}\,\ell)\\
p\nmid m\end{subarray}}\left(1+\frac{\ell-1}{p^{s}}\right)=\prod_{\begin{subarray}{c}p\equiv 1\,(\mathrm{mod}\,\ell)\\
p\mid m\end{subarray}}\left(1+\frac{\ell-1}{p^{s}}\right)^{-1}\zeta_{K}(s)F_{\ell,2}(s).

We apply Perron’s formula

\sum_{n\leq X}a_{\ell}(n)=\frac{1}{2\pi i}\int_{1+\epsilon-iT}^{1+\epsilon+iT}\prod_{\begin{subarray}{c}p\equiv 1\,(\mathrm{mod}\,\ell)\\
p\mid m\end{subarray}}\left(1+\frac{\ell-1}{p^{s}}\right)^{-1}\zeta_{K}(s)F_{\ell,2}(s)\frac{X^{s}}{s}ds+O\left(\frac{X^{1+\epsilon}}{T}\right),

and we move the integral to
s=\frac{1}{2}+\epsilon
as before. The bounds are the same, except that we have the extra factor

\Bigg|\prod_{\begin{subarray}{c}p\equiv 1\,(\mathrm{mod}\,\ell)\\
p\mid m\end{subarray}}\left(1+\frac{\ell-1}{p^{s}}\right)^{-1}\Bigg|\ll\ell^{\omega(m)}.

Finally, we obtain,

\displaystyle\sum_{\chi\in\mathcal{F}_{\ell}(X)}\chi(m)=\prod_{\begin{subarray}{c}p\equiv 1\,(\mathrm{mod}\,\ell)\\
p\mid m\end{subarray}}\left(\frac{p}{p+\ell-1}\right)r_{K}\,F_{\ell,2}(1)\,X+O\left(\ell^{\omega(m)}X^{\frac{\ell+2}{\ell+5}+\epsilon}\right).

As before, assuming the Lindelöf Hypothesis, the error term can be improved to
O\left(\ell^{\omega(m)}X^{\frac{1}{2}+\epsilon}\right)
.

∎

Notice that the main term in (
2.2
) represents the expected value of the random variable
\mathbb{X}(m)
defined for primes
p=\ell
and
p\not\equiv 1\,(\mathrm{mod}\,\ell)
by

\mathbb{X}(p)=\omega_{\ell}^{k}\mbox{ with probability}=\textstyle{\frac{1}{\ell}},\;\mbox{for $0\leq k\leq\ell-1$},

and for
p\equiv 1\,(\mathrm{mod}\,\ell)
by

\mathbb{X}(p)=\begin{cases}0&\mbox{with probability}=\frac{\ell-1}{p+\ell-1}\\
\omega_{\ell}^{k}&\mbox{with probability}=\frac{p}{\ell(p+\ell-1)},\;\mbox{for $0\leq k\leq\ell-1$},\par\end{cases}

and extended multiplicatively as

\mathbb{X}(n)=\mathbb{X}(p_{1})^{a_{1}}\mathbb{X}(p_{2})^{a_{2}}\cdots\mathbb{X}(p_{k})^{a_{k}},

for
n=p_{1}^{a_{1}}p_{2}^{a_{2}}\cdots p_{k}^{a_{k}}
.
Specializing (
2.2
) to
m=p^{\ell}
where
p
is a prime, we find that
\chi(p^{\ell})
is, on average over
\chi
, asymptotic to
1
if
p\not\equiv 1\,(\mathrm{mod}\,\ell)
and to
p/(p+\ell-1)
otherwise. Now observe that
\chi(p^{\ell})={\bf 1}_{\chi(p)\not=0}
, so that (
2.2
) can be interpreted as “
\chi(p)
is zero with probability
1-{\bf 1}_{p\equiv 1\,(\mathrm{mod}\,\ell)}(\ell-1)/(p+\ell-1)
”. Our choice of
\mathbb{X}(p)
is the unique choice taking values in
\{0\}\cup\{\omega_{\ell}^{k}\,:\,k\}
with the property that
\mathbb{X}(p)=0
happens with the same probabilty as
\chi(p)=0
, and
\mathbb{P}(\mathbb{X}(p)=\omega_{\ell}^{k})
is independent of
k
.
This justifies the definition of the random variables.

We can also give a simple heuristic for the
random variables, independently of Proposition
2.1
. The following argument is also found in Lemma 8.1 of
[
BDFL10
]
for the function field case.

The heuristic is as follows.
From the definition of
\mathcal{F}_{\ell}
given by (
2.1
), each primitive character of conductor
n
(square-free and supported only on primes which are
\equiv 1\,(\mathrm{mod}\,\ell)
) can be written uniquely as

\chi_{n_{1}}\chi^{2}_{n_{2}}\cdots\chi_{n_{\ell-1}}^{\ell-1}
for
n=n_{1}n_{2}\cdots n_{\ell-1}
. Then, to count the characters, we can count the
(\ell-1)
-tuples
(n_{1},n_{2},\dots,n_{\ell-1})
(supported on primes which are
\equiv 1\,(\mathrm{mod}\,\ell)
) which are both square-free and pairwise coprime.

We create a model modulo
p^{2}
for a fixed prime
p
. In order to count the characters, we want to count the
(\ell-1)
-tuples
(n_{1},n_{2},\dots,n_{\ell-1})\,(\mathrm{mod}\,p^{2})
such that
n_{k}
is not equivalent to
0\,(\mathrm{mod}\,p^{2})
(this models the square-free condition), and
p
does not simultaneously divide any pair of
n_{j}
and
n_{k}
(this models the coprimality condition).
Now we focus on the additional condition that the character is nonzero. For that, we consider
\chi(p^{\ell})=\chi_{n_{1}}(p^{\ell})\chi_{n_{2}}^{2}(p^{\ell})\cdots\chi_{n_{\ell-1}}^{\ell-1}(p^{\ell})
, as this character only takes the values 0 and 1. We need to count the
(\ell-1)
-tuples
(n_{1},n_{2},\dots,n_{\ell-1})\,(\mathrm{mod}\,p^{2})
such that
n_{k}
is not equivalent to
0\,(\mathrm{mod}\,p^{2})
and such that
n_{k}\not\equiv 0\,(\mathrm{mod}\,p)
(this models the fact that the character is not zero).
Then, a model for
{\mathbb{P}}\left({\mathbb{X}}(p)\neq 0\right)=1-{\mathbb{P}}\left({\mathbb{X}}(p)=0\right)
yielding the product on the left side of (
2.2
) is given by
the quotient

\displaystyle\frac{\#\{(n_{1},\dots,n_{\ell-1})\,(\mathrm{mod}\,p^{2})\;:\;n_{k}\not\equiv 0\,(\mathrm{mod}\,p^{2})\forall k,\left(n_{k}\not\equiv 0\,(\mathrm{mod}\,p)\forall k\right)\}}{\#\{(n_{1},\dots,n_{\ell-1})\,(\mathrm{mod}\,p^{2})\;:\;n_{k}\not\equiv 0\,(\mathrm{mod}\,p^{2})\forall k,\left(n_{k}\equiv 0\,(\mathrm{mod}\,p)\mbox{ for at most one value of }k\right)\}\}}.

In the case where
p\not\equiv 1\,(\mathrm{mod}\,\ell)
or
p=\ell
, since the
n_{k}
are supported on primes that are
\equiv 1\,(\mathrm{mod}\,\ell)
, the conditions
n_{k}\equiv 0\,(\mathrm{mod}\,p)
are never met, and the above quotient has the same numerator and denominator,
which gives
{\mathbb{P}}\left({\mathbb{X}}(p)=0\right)=0
.

In the case where
p\equiv 1\,(\mathrm{mod}\,\ell)
, the fact that the
n_{k}
are supported only on primes that are
\equiv 1\,(\mathrm{mod}\,\ell)
is not a restriction as they cover all the residue classes mod
p^{2}
by the Chinese Remainder Theorem and Dirichlet’s Theorem for primes in arithmetic progressions. A simple counting argument then gives

\displaystyle\frac{\#\{(n_{1},\dots,n_{\ell-1})\,(\mathrm{mod}\,p^{2})\;:\;n_{k}\not\equiv 0\,(\mathrm{mod}\,p^{2})\forall k,\left(n_{k}\not\equiv 0\,(\mathrm{mod}\,p)\forall k\right)\}}{\#\{(n_{1},\dots,n_{\ell-1})\,(\mathrm{mod}\,p^{2})\;:\;n_{k}\not\equiv 0\,(\mathrm{mod}\,p^{2})\forall k,\left(n_{k}\equiv 0\,(\mathrm{mod}\,p)\mbox{ for at most one value of }k\right)\}\}}

\displaystyle=\frac{(p^{2}-p)^{\ell-1}}{(p^{2}-p)^{\ell-1}+(\ell-1)(p^{2}-p)^{\ell-2}(p-1)}=\frac{p^{2}-p}{(p^{2}-p)+(\ell-1)(p-1)}=\frac{p}{p+\ell-1},

and this gives
{\mathbb{P}}\left({\mathbb{X}}(p)=0\right)=\frac{\ell-1}{p+\ell-1}
.

3.
Moments of
L(1,\chi)

As we mentioned in the introduction, the proof of Theorem
1.3
relies on comparing the moments of
|L(1,\chi)|^{2z}
for
\chi\in\mathcal{F}_{3}(X)
, to the expectations
\mathbb{E}(|L(1,\mathbb{X})|^{2z})
. We compute these moments in this section.

In order to properly state our result, we introduce the generalized divisor function. Let
z\in{\mathbb{C}}
, and
n
be a positive integer.
The
z
-divisor function
d_{z}(n)
is the multiplicative function defined on primes powers by

(3.1)

\displaystyle d_{z}(p^{a})=\frac{\Gamma(z+a)}{\Gamma(z)\;a!}.

Then, for any Dirichlet series
D(s)=\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}\frac{a(n)}{n^{s}}
, where the
a(n)
are completely multiplicative, and for
s\in{\mathbb{C}}
such that the series is absolutely convergent, we have

D(s)^{z}=\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}\frac{d_{z}(n)a(n)}{n^{s}}.

We collect here the estimates that we will use for
d_{z}(n)
, which can also be found at the end of Section 2 of
[
GS03
]
. We have
|d_{z}(n)|\leq d_{|z|}(n)
, and for a real number
k\geq 1
,
d_{k}(mn)\leq d_{k}(m)d_{k}(n)
.
For any positive integers
a,b,n
, we have
d_{a}(n)d_{b}(n)\leq d_{a+b}(n)
, and for any
z\in{\mathbb{C}},\beta\in{\mathbb{R}}
,
|d_{z}(n)|^{\beta}\leq d_{|z|^{\beta}}(n)
.

The goal of this section is to prove the following result.

Theorem 3.1
.

Let
z
be a complex number and
X
be large. Then, uniformly in the region
|z|\leq\frac{\log X}{16\log_{2}X\log_{3}X}
we have

\displaystyle\frac{1}{|\mathcal{F}_{3}(X)|}\sum_{\chi\in\mathcal{F}_{3}(X)}|L(1,\chi)|^{2z}

\displaystyle=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}n,m\geqslant 1\\
nm^{2}=\hbox to5.21pt{\vbox to5.21pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 0.2pt\lower-0.2pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}

\lxSVG@closescope }}}

\lxSVG@closescope }}}
{}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{{}}{}{}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}

\lxSVG@closescope
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}\end{subarray}}\frac{d_{z}(m)d_{z}(n)}{mn}\prod_{\begin{subarray}{c}p\equiv 1\,(\mathrm{mod}\,3)\\
p\mid nm\end{subarray}}\frac{p}{p+2}+O\left(\exp\left(-\frac{\log X}{16\log_{2}X}\right)\right)

\displaystyle={\mathbb{E}}(|L(1,{\mathbb{X}})|^{2z})+O\left(\exp\left(-\frac{\log X}{16\log_{2}X}\right)\right),

where the
z
-divisor function
d_{z}(n)
is the multiplicative function defined by (
3.1
) and the
notation indicates that the sum is restricted to the case where
nm^{2}
is a perfect cube.

We remark that the range in Theorem
3.1
is more limited than what is provided in
[
GS03
, Theorem 3]
, as the authors are able to take
|z|\leqslant\frac{\log X\log_{3}X}{e^{12}\log_{2}X}
. A key input of their argument is a
result of Graham and Ringrose
[
GR90
, Theorem 5]
that is only available over
{\mathbb{Q}}
. For cubic characters, one needs to work over
{\mathbb{Q}}(\omega_{3})
.

We first remark that the second identity of Theorem
3.1
follows directly since

\displaystyle{\mathbb{E}}(|L(1,{\mathbb{X}})|^{2z})

\displaystyle=\sum_{n,m=1}^{\infty}\frac{d_{z}(n)d_{z}(m)}{nm}{\mathbb{E}}\left({\mathbb{X}}(n)\overline{{\mathbb{X}}}(m)\right)=\sum_{n,m=1}^{\infty}\frac{d_{z}(n)d_{z}(m)}{nm}{\mathbb{E}}\left({\mathbb{X}}(nm^{2})\right)

\displaystyle=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}n,m=1\\
nm^{2}=\hbox to5.21pt{\vbox to5.21pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 0.2pt\lower-0.2pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}

p\mid mn\end{subarray}}\frac{p}{p+2},

where the last line follows from Lemma
4.2
.

Before proceeding to the proof of Theorem
3.1
, we require a few auxiliary results.
We first recall the following definition

(3.2)

\displaystyle N(\sigma,T,\chi):=\#\{\rho=\beta+i\gamma:L(\rho,\chi)=0,\;\sigma\leq\beta<1\text{ and }|\gamma|\leq T\}.

Estimates for
N(\sigma,T,\chi)
are known as zero density estimates. The Generalized Riemann Hypothesis states that for any
\sigma>\frac{1}{2}
we have
N(\sigma,T,\chi)=0
for all characters
\chi
modulo
q
. In the absence of the Riemann Hypothesis one can show that
N(\sigma,T,\chi)
is quite small when compared to the total zero count

N(T,\chi):=\#\{\rho=\beta+i\gamma:L(\rho,\chi)=0,\;0<\beta<1\text{ and }|\gamma|\leq T\}\sim\frac{T}{\pi}\log T.

Lemma 3.2
.

[
Mon71
, Theorem 12.2]

Suppose that
Q\geq 1
and
T\geqslant 2
. If
\frac{1}{2}\leq\sigma\leq\frac{4}{5}
, we have

\sum_{q\leq Q}\,\,\sideset{}{{}^{*}}{\sum}_{\chi\,(\mathrm{mod}\,q)}N(\sigma,T,\chi)\ll(Q^{2}T)^{\frac{3(1-\sigma)}{2-\sigma}}(\log(QT))^{9},

and if
\frac{4}{5}\leq\sigma\leq 1
then

\sum_{q\leq Q}\,\,\sideset{}{{}^{*}}{\sum}_{\chi\,(\mathrm{mod}\,q)}N(\sigma,T,\chi)\ll(Q^{2}T)^{\frac{2(1-\sigma)}{\sigma}}(\log(QT))^{14}.

Note that the power of
T
here is strictly less than 1 for all
\frac{1}{2}<\sigma\leq 1
. We will see why this becomes useful in just a few lemmas. First, let us write an expression for
|L(1,\chi)|^{2z}
under the assumption that
N(1-\epsilon,2(\log q)^{2/\epsilon},\chi)=0
for a fixed
\epsilon
:

Lemma 3.3
.

Let
q
be large and
0<\epsilon<\frac{1}{2}
be fixed. Let
y
be a real number such that
\frac{\log q}{\log_{2}q}\leq\log y\leq\log q
. Furthermore, assume that
L(s,\chi)

has no zeros inside the rectangle
\{s:1-\epsilon<\re(s)\leq 1\,\mathrm{and}\,|\im(s)|\leq 2(\log q)^{2/\epsilon}\}
. Then for any complex number
z
such that
|z|\leq\log y/(4\log_{2}q\log_{3}q)
we have

\displaystyle|L(1,\chi)|^{2z}=\sum_{m,n\geqslant 1}\frac{d_{z}(m)d_{z}(n)\chi(n)\overline{\chi}(m)}{mn}e^{-mn/y}+O_{\epsilon}\left(\exp\left(-\frac{\log y}{4\log_{2}q}\right)\right),

where
d_{z}(n)
is the
z
-divisor function defined by (
3.1
).

Proof.

The proof can be easily adapted from the proof of Proposition 3.3 in
[
DL18
]
.
∎

We will need two versions of the Pólya–Vinogradov inequality.

Lemma 3.4
.

[
Hin83
]
Let
K
be a number field of degree
d
and let
\chi
be a primitive nonprincipal character of the group of narrow ideal-classes modulo an ideal
\mathfrak{q}
. Then we have

\sum_{N(\mathfrak{a})\leq X}\chi(\mathfrak{a})\ll N(\mathfrak{q})^{\frac{1}{d+1}}(\log N(\mathfrak{q}))^{d}X^{\frac{d-1}{d+1}}.

Lemma 3.5
.

If
n
is a positive integer which is not a cube, then

\sum_{\chi\in\mathcal{F}_{3}(X)}\chi(n)\ll X^{\frac{1}{2}}\,\log{X}\,\tilde{n}^{\frac{1}{2}}\,(\log{\tilde{n}})^{\frac{3}{2}},

where
\tilde{n}
denotes the radical of
n
.

Remark 3.6
.

This result is a weaker analogue of
[
GS03
, Lemma 4.1]
, which gives a bound of
X^{\frac{1}{2}}n^{\frac{1}{4}}(\log{n})^{\frac{1}{2}}
in the quadratic case. This is because we have to use Lemma
3.4
, since we are working with integers in
{\mathbb{Z}}[\omega_{3}]
, and the degree of the extension is
2
.

The following statement provides a way to express the number of elements of
\mathcal{F}_{3}(X)
as a single sum over characters of
{\mathbb{Z}}[\omega_{3}]
, with some additional conditions.

Lemma 3.7
.

[
BY10
, Lemma 2.1]

The primitive cubic Dirichlet characters of conductor
q
,
(q,3)=1
are of the form
\chi_{n}:m\to\left(\frac{m}{n}\right)_{3}
for some
n\in{\mathbb{Z}}[\omega_{3}]
,
n\equiv 1\,(\mathrm{mod}\,3)
,
n
squarefree and not divisible by any rational primes, with norm
N(n)=q
.

From Lemma
3.7
, we have

\sum_{\chi\in\mathcal{F}_{3}(X)}1=\sideset{}{{}^{\prime}}{\sum}_{\begin{subarray}{c}n\in{\mathbb{Z}}[\omega_{3}]\\
N(n)\leq X\end{subarray}}1,

where the prime indicates that the sum runs over the integers
n\in{\mathbb{Z}}[\omega_{3}]
which are square-free, not divisible by any
p\in{\mathbb{Z}}
, and such that
n\equiv 1\,(\mathrm{mod}\,3)
. We use the detectors

\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}d\in{\mathbb{Z}},d\mid n\\
d\equiv 1\,(\mathrm{mod}\,3)\end{subarray}}\mu_{\mathbb{Z}}(d)=\begin{cases}1&\text{if $n$ is not divisible by a rational prime},\\
0&\mbox{otherwise},\end{cases}

(where
\mu_{\mathbb{Z}}(d)=\mu(|d|)
), and

\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}d\in{\mathbb{Z}}[\omega_{3}],d\equiv 1\,(\mathrm{mod}\,3)\\
d^{2}\mid n\end{subarray}}\mu_{{\mathbb{Z}}[\omega_{3}]}(d)=\begin{cases}1&\text{if $n$ is square-free},\\
0&\mbox{otherwise}.\end{cases}

Proof of Lemma
3.5
.

Using Lemma
3.7
, we have

(3.3)

\displaystyle\sum_{\chi\in\mathcal{F}_{3}(X)}\chi(n)=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}d\in{\mathbb{Z}},\;d\equiv 1\,(\mathrm{mod}\,3)\\
|d|\leq\sqrt{X}\end{subarray}}\mu_{\mathbb{Z}}(d)\left(\frac{n}{d}\right)_{3}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\ell\in{\mathbb{Z}}[\omega_{3}],\;\ell\equiv 1\,(\mathrm{mod}\,3)\\
(\ell,d)=1\\
N(\ell)\leq\sqrt{X/N(d)}\end{subarray}}\mu_{{\mathbb{Z}}[\omega_{3}]}(\ell)\left(\frac{n}{\ell^{2}}\right)_{3}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}c\in{\mathbb{Z}}[\omega_{3}],\;c\equiv 1\,(\mathrm{mod}\,3)\\
(c,d)=1\\
N(c)\leq X/N(d\ell^{2})\end{subarray}}\left(\frac{n}{c}\right)_{3}

\displaystyle=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}d\in{\mathbb{Z}},\;d\equiv 1\,(\mathrm{mod}\,3)\\
e\mid d,\;e\equiv 1\,(\mathrm{mod}\,3)\\
|d|\leq\sqrt{X}\end{subarray}}\mu_{{\mathbb{Z}}[\omega_{3}]}(e)\mu_{\mathbb{Z}}(d)\left(\frac{n}{d}\right)_{3}\left(\frac{n}{e}\right)_{3}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\ell\in{\mathbb{Z}}[\omega_{3}],\;\ell\equiv 1\,(\mathrm{mod}\,3)\\
(\ell,d)=1\\
N(\ell)\leq\sqrt{X/N(d)}\end{subarray}}\mu_{{\mathbb{Z}}[\omega_{3}]}(\ell)\left(\frac{n}{\ell^{2}}\right)_{3}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}c\in{\mathbb{Z}}[\omega_{3}],\;c\equiv 1\,(\mathrm{mod}\,3)\\
N(c)\leq X/N(de\ell^{2})\end{subarray}}\left(\frac{n}{c}\right)_{3}.

The function
\psi_{n}:(c)\mapsto\left(\frac{n}{c}\right)_{3}
defined on ideals
(c)\subset{\mathbb{Z}}[\omega_{3}]
(coprime to 3, and where
c\equiv 1\,(\mathrm{mod}\,3)
) is a non-trivial Hecke character of modulus
9n
, and
we apply Lemma
3.4
. It is not necessarily primitive, but we work with the primitive character induced by
\left(\frac{n}{c}\right)_{3}
, whose conductor divides
9\tilde{n}
, where
\tilde{n}
is the radical of
n
. Since
[{\mathbb{Q}}(\omega_{3}):{\mathbb{Q}}]=2
, we get by Lemma
3.4
that

\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}c\in{\mathbb{Z}}[\omega_{3}],c\equiv 1\,(\mathrm{mod}\,3)\\
N(c)\leq X/N(de\ell^{2})\end{subarray}}\left(\frac{n}{c}\right)_{3}\ll N(\tilde{n})^{\frac{1}{3}}\log^{2}{N(\tilde{n})}\left(\frac{X}{N(de\ell^{2})}\right)^{\frac{1}{3}}\ll\tilde{n}^{\frac{2}{3}}\left(\frac{X}{N(de\ell^{2})}\right)^{\frac{1}{3}}(\log{\tilde{n}})^{2},

and then

\displaystyle\sum_{\chi\in\mathcal{F}_{3}(X)}\chi(n)

\displaystyle\ll\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}d\in{\mathbb{Z}},\;d\equiv 1\,(\mathrm{mod}\,3)\\
|d|\leq\sqrt{X}\\
e\mid d,\;e\equiv 1\,(\mathrm{mod}\,3)\end{subarray}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\ell\in{\mathbb{Z}}[\omega_{3}],\ell\equiv 1\,(\mathrm{mod}\,3)\\
(\ell,d)=1\\
N(\ell)\leq\sqrt{X/N(d)}\end{subarray}}\min\left(\tilde{n}^{\frac{2}{3}}\left(\frac{X}{N(de\ell^{2})}\right)^{\frac{1}{3}}(\log{\tilde{n}})^{2},\frac{X}{N(ed\ell^{2})}\right)

\displaystyle\ll X^{\frac{1}{3}}\tilde{n}^{\frac{2}{3}}(\log{\tilde{n}})^{2}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}d\in{\mathbb{Z}},d\equiv 1\,(\mathrm{mod}\,3)\\
|d|\leq\sqrt{X}\\
e\mid d,\;e\equiv 1\,(\mathrm{mod}\,3)\end{subarray}}\frac{1}{N(ed)^{\frac{1}{3}}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\ell\in{\mathbb{Z}}[\omega_{3}],\ell\equiv 1\,(\mathrm{mod}\,3)\\
N(\ell)\leq\ell_{0}\end{subarray}}\frac{1}{N(\ell)^{\frac{2}{3}}}

\displaystyle+X\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}d\in{\mathbb{Z}},d\equiv 1\,(\mathrm{mod}\,3)\\
|d|\leq\sqrt{X}\\
e\mid d,\;e\equiv 1\,(\mathrm{mod}\,3)\end{subarray}}\frac{1}{N(ed)}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\ell\in{\mathbb{Z}}[\omega_{3}],\ell\equiv 1\,(\mathrm{mod}\,3)\\
N(\ell)\geq\ell_{0}\end{subarray}}\frac{1}{N(\ell)^{2}}

\displaystyle\ll X^{\frac{1}{2}}\tilde{n}^{\frac{1}{2}}(\log{\tilde{n}})^{\frac{3}{2}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}d\in{\mathbb{Z}},d\equiv 1\,(\mathrm{mod}\,3)\\
|d|\leq\sqrt{X}\\
e\mid d,\;e\equiv 1\,(\mathrm{mod}\,3)\end{subarray}}\frac{1}{N(ed)^{\frac{1}{2}}}\ll X^{\frac{1}{2}}\log X\tilde{n}^{\frac{1}{2}}(\log{\tilde{n}})^{\frac{3}{2}},

where
\ell_{0}=X^{\frac{1}{2}}\tilde{n}^{-1/2}(\log\tilde{n})^{-3/2}N(ed)^{-1/2}
.
∎

Proof of Theorem
3.1
.

Let
y=X^{\alpha}
for some
\alpha<\frac{1}{2}
and let
z
be a complex number such that
|z|\leq\log y/(4\log_{2}X\log_{3}X)
.
Let

\widetilde{\mathcal{F}}_{\epsilon}(X)=\{\chi\in\mathcal{F}_{3}(X):N(1-\epsilon,2(\log q)^{2/\epsilon},\chi)=0\},

where
N(1-\epsilon,2(\log q)^{2/\epsilon},\chi)
is defined by (
3.2
). Then,

\frac{1}{|\mathcal{F}_{3}(X)|}\sum_{\chi\in\mathcal{F}_{3}(X)}|L(1,\chi)|^{2z}=\frac{1}{|\mathcal{F}_{3}(X)|}\sum_{\chi\in\widetilde{\mathcal{F}}_{\epsilon}(X)}|L(1,\chi)|^{2z}+\frac{1}{|\mathcal{F}_{3}(X)|}\sum_{\chi\in\mathcal{F}_{3}(X)\setminus\widetilde{\mathcal{F}}_{\epsilon}(X)}|L(1,\chi)|^{2z}.

We first show that the second sum is negligible using Lemma
3.2
. Afterwards, we apply Lemma
3.3
to the first sum to obtain the desired main term.
First, if
\frac{4}{5}\leq 1-\epsilon
then

\displaystyle|\mathcal{F}_{3}(X)\setminus\widetilde{\mathcal{F}}_{\epsilon}(X)|

\displaystyle=\sum_{q\leq X}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\chi\text{ cubic}\\
N(1-\epsilon,2(\log q)^{2/\epsilon},\chi)\neq 0\end{subarray}}1

\displaystyle\ll\sum_{q\leq X}\,\,\sideset{}{{}^{*}}{\sum}_{\chi\,(\mathrm{mod}\,q)}N(1-\epsilon,2(\log q)^{2/\epsilon},\chi)

\displaystyle\ll(X^{2}2(\log X)^{2/\epsilon})^{\frac{2\epsilon}{1-\epsilon}}(\log(X2(\log X)^{2/\epsilon}))^{14}\ll X^{\frac{4}{5}+\epsilon},

where the last line comes from choosing
1
1

1

It suffices to choose any
\epsilon<\frac{1}{5}
. The choice of
\epsilon=\frac{1}{6}
is motivated by convenience of the final expression.

\epsilon=\frac{1}{6}
.
Thus we have, by the standard bound
|L(1,\chi)|\ll\log\mathrm{cond}(\chi)
,

\sum_{\chi\in\mathcal{F}_{3}(X)\setminus\widetilde{\mathcal{F}}_{\epsilon}(X)}|L(1,\chi)|^{2z}\ll X^{\frac{4}{5}+\epsilon}\exp(2z\log_{2}X).

Recall that
y=X^{\alpha}
, for some
0<\alpha<\frac{1}{2}
, which gives

\sum_{\chi\in\mathcal{F}_{3}(X)\setminus\widetilde{\mathcal{F}}_{\epsilon}(X)}|L(1,\chi)|^{2z}\ll X^{\frac{4}{5}+\epsilon}\exp\left(\frac{\alpha\log X}{2\log_{3}X}\right)\ll X^{\frac{4}{5}+2\epsilon}.

So far we have seen,

\frac{1}{|\mathcal{F}_{3}(X)|}\sum_{\chi\in\mathcal{F}_{3}(X)}|L(1,\chi)|^{2z}=\frac{1}{|\mathcal{F}_{3}(X)|}\sum_{\chi\in\widetilde{\mathcal{F}}_{1/6}(X)}|L(1,\chi)|^{2z}+O\left(X^{-\frac{1}{5}+\epsilon}\right).

Now, we apply Lemma
3.3
to obtain

\displaystyle\frac{1}{|\mathcal{F}_{3}(X)|}\sum_{\chi\in\widetilde{\mathcal{F}}_{{1/6}}(X)}|L(1,\chi)|^{2z}=\frac{1}{|\mathcal{F}_{3}(X)|}\sum_{\chi\in\widetilde{\mathcal{F}}_{1/6}(X)}\sum_{m,n\geqslant 1}\frac{d_{z}(m)d_{z}(n)\chi(n)\overline{\chi}(m)}{mn}e^{-mn/y}

\displaystyle+O\left(\exp\left(-\frac{\alpha\log X}{4\log_{2}q}\right)\right).

In order to apply the orthogonality relation we require the sum to be over
\mathcal{F}_{3}(X)
and not
\widetilde{\mathcal{F}}_{1/6}(X)
. We can extend our sum to the full family by noting that

\displaystyle\sum_{\chi\in\mathcal{F}_{3}(X)\setminus\widetilde{\mathcal{F}}_{1/6}(X)}\sum_{m,n\geqslant 1}\frac{d_{z}(m)d_{z}(n)\chi(n)\overline{\chi}(m)}{mn}e^{-mn/y}

\displaystyle\ll|\mathcal{F}_{3}(X)\setminus\widetilde{\mathcal{F}}_{1/6}(X)|\sum_{m,n\geqslant 1}\frac{d_{z}(m)d_{z}(n)}{mn}e^{-mn/y}

\displaystyle\ll X^{\frac{4}{5}+\epsilon}(\log(3y))^{2\lceil|z|\rceil}\ll X^{\frac{4}{5}+2\epsilon},

where the second line follows from
[
GS03
, Eq. (2.4)]
and the fact that
e^{-mn/y}\leq e^{-(m+n)/y}
for
m,n>1
,
and the last calculation comes from the assumptions on
y
and
|z|
.

Therefore,

\displaystyle\frac{1}{|\mathcal{F}_{3}(X)|}\sum_{\chi\in\mathcal{F}_{3}(X)}

\displaystyle|L(1,\chi)|^{2z}=\sum_{m,n\geqslant 1}\frac{d_{z}(m)d_{z}(n)}{mn}e^{-mn/y}\frac{1}{|\mathcal{F}_{3}(X)|}\sum_{\chi\in{\mathcal{F}}_{3}(X)}\chi(nm^{2})+O\left(\exp\left(-\frac{\alpha\log X}{4\log_{2}q}\right)\right),

and applying (
2.2
), we have

\displaystyle\frac{1}{|\mathcal{F}_{3}(X)|}\sum_{\chi\in\mathcal{F}_{3}(X)}|L(1,\chi)|^{2z}=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}m,n\geqslant 1\\
nm^{2}=\hbox to5.21pt{\vbox to5.21pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 0.2pt\lower-0.2pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}
{}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{{}}{}{}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}
{}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{{}}{}{}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}
{}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{{}}{}{}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}
{}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{{}}{}{}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}

for

positive integers.

{}{}{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 1.54 6.65 L 6.65 6.65}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
{{}}{}{{}}{}{{}}
{}{}{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 5.12 5.12 L 6.65 6.65}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
{{}}{}{{}}{}{{}}
{}{}{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 5.12 0 L 6.65 1.54}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
{{}}{}{{}}{}{{}}
{}{}{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 6.65 1.54 L 6.65 6.65}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
{{}}{}{{}}{}{{}}
{}{}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@setdash{0.4pt,1.0pt}{0.0pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-dasharray,stroke-dashoffset} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 1.54 1.54 L 6.65 1.54}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}\end{subarray}}\frac{d_{z}(m)d_{z}(n)}{mn}e^{-mn/y}\sum_{\chi\in{\mathcal{F}_{3}}(X)}\chi(nm^{2})\ll X^{\frac{1}{2}}\log X\bigg(\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}n\geqslant 1\end{subarray}}\frac{d_{z}(n)(\log(n))^{\frac{3}{2}}}{\sqrt{n}}e^{-n/y}\bigg)^{2}.

We now further split (
3.4
) into two sums, for
n\leq y(\log y)^{2}
and for
n>y(\log y)^{2}
. For the first case, using
1\leq\frac{\sqrt{y}\log(y)}{\sqrt{n}}
, we get

\displaystyle\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}n\leq y(\log y)^{2}\end{subarray}}\frac{d_{z}(n)(\log n)^{\frac{3}{2}}}{\sqrt{n}}e^{-n/y}\leq\sqrt{y}(\log y)^{3}\sum_{n\leq y(\log y)^{2}}\frac{d_{z}(n)}{n}e^{-n/y}\ll\sqrt{y}(\log y)^{3}(\log(3y))^{\lceil|z|\rceil}.

Replacing in (
3.4
) and using
y=X^{\alpha}
we see the contribution of
n\leq y(\log y)^{2}
is

\displaystyle\ll X^{\frac{1}{2}+\alpha}(\log X)^{7}\exp\left(\frac{\alpha}{2}\frac{\log(X)}{\log_{3}X}\right)\ll X^{\frac{1}{2}+\alpha+\epsilon},

which is
O\left(X^{1-\epsilon^{\prime}}\right)
when
\alpha<\frac{1}{2}
.
For the remaining range, we note that if
m\geq y(\log y)^{2}
then
e^{-m/(2y)}\leq e^{-(\log y)^{2}/2}
, so that

\displaystyle\Bigg(\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}n\geq y(\log y)^{2}\end{subarray}}\frac{d_{z}(n)(\log(n))^{\frac{3}{2}}}{\sqrt{n}}e^{-n/y}\Bigg)^{2}\ll e^{-(\log y)^{2}}(\log X)^{3}\Bigg(\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}n\geq y(\log y)^{2}\end{subarray}}\frac{d_{z}(n)}{\sqrt{n}}e^{-n/(2y)}\Bigg)^{2}.

Now, if
k
is a positive integer and
x>3
, we have
d_{k}(n)e^{-n/x}\leq e^{k/x}\sum_{a_{1}a_{2}\cdots a_{k}=n}e^{-(a_{1}+a_{2}+\cdots+a_{k})/x}
so that

\displaystyle\Bigg(\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}n\geq y(\log y)^{2}\end{subarray}}\frac{d_{z}(n)}{\sqrt{n}}e^{-n/(2y)}\Bigg)^{2}\leq\Bigg(e^{1/(2y)}\sum_{a=1}^{\infty}\frac{e^{-a/(2y)}}{a^{\frac{1}{2}}}\Bigg)^{2\lceil|z|\rceil}\ll y^{2\lceil|z|\rceil}.

Replacing in (
3.4
), we see the contribution of
n>y(\log y)^{2}
is

\displaystyle\ll X^{\frac{1}{2}}(\log X)^{4}e^{-(\log y)^{2}}y^{2\lceil|z|\rceil}

\displaystyle=\exp\left(\frac{1}{2}\log X+4\log_{2}X-(\log y)^{2}+2\lceil|z|\rceil\log y\right)

\displaystyle=\exp\left(\frac{1}{2}\log X+4\log_{2}X-(\alpha\log X)^{2}+\frac{\alpha^{2}(\log X)^{2}}{2\log_{2}X\log_{3}X}\right)

\displaystyle\ll X^{\frac{1}{2}+\epsilon}.

Thus, we have proven that

(3.5)

\displaystyle\frac{1}{|\mathcal{F}_{3}(X)|}\sum_{\chi\in\mathcal{F}_{3}(X)}|L(1,\chi)|^{2z}

\displaystyle=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}m,n\geqslant 1\\

{}{}{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 9.06 L 2.72 11.77}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
{{}}{}{{}}{}{{}}
{}{}{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 2.72 11.77 L 11.77 11.77}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
{{}}{}{{}}{}{{}}
{}{}{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 9.06 9.06 L 11.77 11.77}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
{{}}{}{{}}{}{{}}
{}{}{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 9.06 0 L 11.77 2.72}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
{{}}{}{{}}{}{{}}
{}{}{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 11.77 2.72 L 11.77 11.77}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
{{}}{}{{}}{}{{}}
{}{}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@setdash{0.4pt,1.0pt}{0.0pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-dasharray,stroke-dashoffset} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 2.72 2.72 L 11.77 2.72}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}
and the substitution
n=rs^{3}
and
m=rt^{3}
. For the sum over
r
, we use
[
Lam11
, Lemma 3.3]
, with the choice
\beta=\frac{3}{\log_{2}X}
, to obtain

\sum_{r\geq 1}\frac{d_{|z|}(r)^{2}}{r^{2-2\beta}}\leq\exp((2+o(1))\lceil|z|\rceil\log_{2}\lceil|z|\rceil).

For the sum over
s
, we use the multiplicativity of
d_{z}(n)
, which gives

\sum_{s=1}^{\infty}\frac{d_{|z|}(s)^{3}}{s^{3-3\beta}}=\prod_{p}\left(1+\frac{d_{|z|}(p)^{3}}{p^{3-3\beta}}+\frac{d_{|z|}(p^{2})^{3}}{p^{6-6\beta}}+\cdots\right),

the recursive property
\Gamma(z+1)=z\Gamma(z)
, and the fact that for any integer
r\geq 1
, we have

d_{|z|}(p^{r})=\frac{\Gamma(|z|+r)}{\Gamma(|z|)\,r!}=\frac{|z|(|z|+1))\cdots(|z|+r-1)}{r!}.

Splitting the Euler product into two pieces depending on the size of
p
relative to
|z|
, we have

\displaystyle\sum_{s=1}^{\infty}\frac{d_{|z|}(s)^{3}}{s^{3-3\beta}}

\displaystyle=\prod_{p\leq|z|+2}\left(1+\frac{d_{|z|}(p)^{3}}{p^{3-3\beta}}+\frac{d_{|z|}(p^{2})^{3}}{p^{6-6\beta}}+\cdots\right)\prod_{p>|z|+2}\left(1+O\left(\frac{|z|^{3}}{p^{3-3\beta}}\right)\right)

\displaystyle\ll\prod_{p\leq|z|+2}\left(1+\frac{d_{|z|}(p)}{p^{1-\beta}}+\frac{d_{|z|}(p^{2})}{p^{2-2\beta}}+\cdots\right)^{3}\prod_{p>|z|+2}\left(1+O\left(\frac{|z|^{3}}{p^{3-3\beta}}\right)\right)

\displaystyle\ll\prod_{p\leq|z|+2}\left(1+\frac{3(|z|+1)}{p^{1-\beta}}+\cdots\right)^{3}\prod_{p>|z|+2}\left(1+O\left(\frac{|z|^{3}}{p^{3-3\beta}}\right)\right)

\displaystyle\ll\prod_{p\leq|z|+2}\left(1-\frac{1}{p^{1-\beta}}\right)^{-3|z|}\prod_{p>|z|+2}\left(1+O\left(\frac{|z|^{3}}{p^{3-3\beta}}\right)\right),

and using Mertens’ theorem
\prod_{p\leq y}\left(1-\frac{1}{p}\right)\sim\frac{e^{-\gamma}}{\log y}
, we obtain

\displaystyle\sum_{s=1}^{\infty}\frac{(d_{|z|}(s))^{3}}{s^{3-3\beta}}

\displaystyle\ll(e^{\gamma}\log(|z|+2))^{3|z|}\prod_{p>|z|+2}\left(1+O\left(\frac{|z|^{3}}{p^{3}}\right)\right)

\displaystyle\ll(e^{2}\log(|z|+2))^{3|z|}.

Replacing in (
3.6
), the error term from removing the smoothing is

\displaystyle\ll\exp(-\beta\log y+12|z|+6|z|\log_{2}|z|+(2+o(1))\lceil|z|\rceil\log_{2}\lceil|z|\rceil))

\displaystyle\ll\exp\left(-\beta\alpha\log X+\frac{3\alpha\log X}{\log_{2}X\log_{3}X}+\frac{3\alpha\log X}{2\log_{2}X\log_{3}X}\log\left(\log_{2}X-\log_{3}X-\log_{4}X\right)\right.

\displaystyle\hskip 72.26999pt+(2+o(1))\frac{\alpha\log X}{4\log_{2}X\log_{3}X}\log\left(\log_{2}X-\log_{3}X-\log_{4}X\right)\Bigg)

\displaystyle\ll\exp\left(-\frac{\alpha\log X}{\log_{2}X}\right),

which is smaller than the error term from (
3.5
).
Recalling that
\alpha<1/2
, we choose
\alpha=1/4
.
This completes the proof.
∎

4.
Working with the random variables

Let
{\mathbb{X}}(m)
and
L(1,{\mathbb{X}})
be the random variables defined in the introduction. The main result of this section is the following theorem, which will be used to prove Theorems
1.2
and
1.3
.

Theorem 4.1
.

For
\tau
large, we have

\displaystyle\Phi(\tau)=\frac{{\mathbb{E}}\left(|L(1,{\mathbb{X}})|^{2\kappa}\right)(e^{\gamma}\tau)^{-2\kappa}}{\kappa\sqrt{2\pi\mathcal{L}^{\prime\prime}(\kappa)}}\left(1+O\left(\sqrt{\frac{\log\kappa}{\kappa}}\right)\right)

and

\Psi(\tau)=\frac{{\mathbb{E}}\left(|L(1,{\mathbb{X}})|^{-2\widetilde{\kappa}}\right)\zeta(3)^{\widetilde{\kappa}}}{\widetilde{\kappa}\sqrt{2\pi\widetilde{\mathcal{L}}^{\prime\prime}(\widetilde{\kappa})}{(e^{\gamma}\tau^{2})^{\widetilde{\kappa}}}}\left(1+O\left(\sqrt{\frac{\log\widetilde{\kappa}}{\widetilde{\kappa}}}\right)\right),

where
\Phi(\tau)
and
\Psi(\tau)
are given by (
1.6
) and (
1.7
) respectively,
\mathcal{L},\widetilde{\mathcal{L}}
are defined by (
4.2
) and (
4.3
), and
\kappa,\widetilde{\kappa}
are the unique solutions to (
4.16
) and (
4.17
).

4.1.
Expectations

Lemma 4.2
.

Let
m=p_{1}^{a_{1}}\cdots p_{k}^{a_{k}}
, and let

be the independent random variables defined in the introduction, taking the value 0 with probability

\delta_{p}:=\begin{cases}0&\text{if}\;p\equiv 2\,(\mathrm{mod}\,3)\;\text{or}\;p=3,\\
\frac{2}{p+2}&\text{if}\;p\equiv 1\,(\mathrm{mod}\,3),\end{cases}

and each of the values
1,\omega_{3},\omega_{3}^{2}
with probability
\frac{\alpha_{p}}{3}
where

(4.1)

\frac{p}{p+2}&\text{if}\;p\equiv 1\,(\mathrm{mod}\,3).\end{cases}

Notice that
\alpha_{p}=1-\delta_{p}
.
Then

\mathbb{E}(\mathbb{X}(m))=\begin{cases}\prod_{j=1}^{k}(1-\delta_{p_{j}})=\prod_{\begin{subarray}{c}p\equiv 1\,(\mathrm{mod}\,3)\\

0&\mbox{otherwise.}\end{cases}

Furthermore,
\sum_{m=1}^{\infty}\mathbb{E}\left(\frac{\mathbb{X}(m)}{m}\right)\leq\zeta(3)
and
\sum_{m=1}^{\infty}\mathrm{Var}\left(\frac{\mathbb{X}(m)}{m}\right)\leq\zeta(6)
.

Proof.

By independence,

\mathbb{E}(\mathbb{X}(m))=\prod_{j=1}^{k}\mathbb{E}(\mathbb{X}(p_{j})^{a_{j}}).

If
a_{j}
is divisible by 3, then

\mathbb{E}(\mathbb{X}(p_{j})^{a_{j}})=\frac{\alpha_{p_{j}}}{3}+\frac{\alpha_{p_{j}}}{3}+\frac{\alpha_{p_{j}}}{3}=1-\delta_{p_{j}}.

On the other hand, if
a_{j}\equiv\pm 1\,(\mathrm{mod}\,3)
,

\mathbb{E}(\mathbb{X}(p_{j})^{a_{j}})=\frac{\alpha_{p_{j}}}{3}+\omega_{3}^{\pm 1}\frac{\alpha_{p_{j}}}{3}+\omega_{3}^{\mp 1}\frac{\alpha_{p_{j}}}{3}=0.

Finally, we have

\sum_{m=1}^{\infty}\mathbb{E}\left(\frac{\mathbb{X}(m)}{m}\right)=\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}\frac{\mathbb{E}(\mathbb{X}(n^{3}))}{n^{3}}\leq\zeta(3),

and

\displaystyle\sum_{m=1}^{\infty}\mathrm{Var}\left(\frac{\mathbb{X}(m)}{m}\right)=

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}\frac{1}{n^{6}}\left[\mathbb{E}\left(\mathbb{X}(n^{3})\right)-\mathbb{E}\left(\mathbb{X}(n^{3})\right)^{2}\right]\leq\zeta(6).
